ParlaMint-ES_2019-02-27-CD190227.u2	We shall now proceed, ladies and gentlemen, to the urgent appearance of the President of the Government at the plenary session of the Congress of Deputies, and the following requests for attendance will be discussed cumulatively: The urgent appearance of the President of the Government at the plenary session of the House, requested by Mrs Montserrat Montserrat and 102 other Members, to explain the reasons why she still holds as ministers and other senior officials of her Government to those people who have used instrumental societies to buy houses or manage their assets with the aim of paying less taxes, contradicting her own publicly expressed view of the immoral use of these practices and committing herself with her voters and with the Spaniards to expel those people from her Executive. Secondly, urgent appearance of the President of the Government before the plenary of the House, requested by Mrs Montserrat Montserrat and another 100 Members, to report on the measures the Government intends to take in the Contingency Plan for the exit of the United Kingdom from the European Union without agreement. Thirdly, urgent appearance of the President of the Government at the plenary session of the House, requested by Mrs Montserrat Montserrat and 103 other Members, to report on the final position of the Government of Spain on the political situation in Venezuela. I give the floor to the President of the Government.
ParlaMint-ES_2019-02-27-CD190227.u3	Thank you, Madam President. Good morning. Ladies and gentlemen, I appear before this House to report on the various matters on the agenda of this sitting to which the President of Congress has referred. First of all, I will refer in this speech to the measures contained in the Contingency Plan on Brexit; then to the assessments related to the management of its assets by members of the Government, and finally to the political situation in Venezuela. Ladies and gentlemen, first of all, I would like to say that this is the last parliamentary appearance in which I will have the honour of participating in this parliamentary term. In a few weeks' time, with the dissolution of the Courts, a new stage will be opened that will lead to the general elections of 28 April, and in them the citizens will have the floor to decide what the future composition of this House will be. This will result in an intense, first-timely-filled legislature. It is the first time that a motion of censure crystallizes a change of government in our country in the terms provided for in article 113 of our Constitution and, therefore, giving it full constitutional legitimacy, and it is also the first time that the Presidency of the House has a sign different from that of the Executive and is led by the main opposition party. Today, ladies and gentlemen, despite the difficulties in reaching agreements, despite the times when noise has overcome the will to build consensus, despite the denaturing of government bodies that have contributed more to the work of the parliamentary blockade than to the dynamism of parliamentary life, I would like to start by calling for parliamentary work. It will be the citizens who, as I said, will have the floor to set up the new composition of this House in two months' time and, whatever the result, we have an obligation to draw the necessary lessons so that the Legislative Power can live up to what citizens demand from it. And I am referring, above all and above all, to the opening up once and for all in our country of an open, diverse, dialogueable, inclusive and tolerant idea of Spain, which must also be reflected in parliamentary debates. Ladies and gentlemen, Spain in this House has to look much more like real Spain, where there is not a single way of feeling Spanish but many. That is the essence of democracy and of whom we defend a Spain in which we all belong and not those who think like us. That is the true spirit of the Constitution that should be preserved as the first rule of the House: to unite the Spaniards and never again to confront the Spaniards. [[Applauds]] Ladies and gentlemen, as you know, the process of negotiating the exit of the United Kingdom from the European Union began on 29 March 2017. It was then that the British Prime Minister, Theresa May, informed the President of the Council of the European Union, Donald Tusk, by letter of her decision to invoke Article 50 of the Treaty on European Union as a result of the referendum held on 23 June 2016 in the United Kingdom on membership of the European Union. I am not going to repeat the arguments that I have already put forward in this House to assess what I consider to be a bad decision, a bad decision; a decision that, however, as the Government of Spain, it is up to us to respect, but which I understand and will always understand as an amendment to the whole common sense, to the logic of the times and to the channel of history. The result of that referendum, ladies and gentlemen - I will not tire of repeating this - is the paradigm of a time when democracy is reduced to the false plebiscite dilemma, to mutually exclusive options, to the simplicity of binary decisions - a yes or a no - as a way to solve hugely complex and nuanced debates. Euroscepticism was always present in British society - that is, we know it - as a latent threat that could revive at any time. However, with the peculiarities of the ever complex link between the United Kingdom and the European Union, this danger could be conjured up by the tacit agreement of the two major British parties, the Conservative Party and the Labour Party. It was the emergency, ladies and gentlemen, of a third actor, the UKIP, of Mr Nigel Farage, of a national-populist, Europhobic and nostalgic court of an era and of an imperial epic, which ended up poisoning this debate with lies, biased information and prejudice. We can therefore draw many lessons from these events arising from the Brexit. The most important, in my opinion, is that the ultra-right does not need to reach power to condition the political agenda, it is enough to condition other actors so that these are the ones who renounce their principles, it is enough to inoculate the virus to other political formations, it is enough to introduce itself as a true Trojan horse in the heart of democracy to boost in months consensuss that it took decades to forge. It was thus that an internal dispute within the Conservative Party ended in an institutional crisis that is far from finding a solution. It was thus that the Trojan horse of the far right ravaged British pragmatism from the very heart of the democratic system itself by hacking democracy with simplistic messages and false disjunctives. Ladies and gentlemen, you cannot compromise with anti-Europeanism, you cannot assume those ultra-right postulates, you cannot, in Churchill's words, reason with the tiger when you have your head in your jaws. And here and in the United Kingdom, as in France and as in other countries of the European Union, that tiger is called ultra-right, and with the ultra-right one cannot go, ladies and gentlemen, nor around the corner. [[Applauds]] A little over a month ago in the European Parliament I called for the need to protect Europe if we wanted Europe to protect its compatriots, the Europeans. One can criticise, ladies and gentlemen, the wrong bet of austerity that the Community institutions made during the economic crisis of the last decade; I have done so, as leader of the opposition. We can criticise the gaps in legitimacy that continue to carry certain Community institutions; I too have done so. We can even censor the slow, exasperating in many cases, with which the process of European construction is progressing; and I too have done so, ladies and gentlemen. But we cannot walk in the opposite direction of history, we cannot deny the usefulness and validity of the European project at this time. That is why it is so necessary to take a step forward to protect a Europe increasingly threatened by the nostalgics of provincial isolationism. We have never been so aware of the entity of the global threats that haunt us. Above borders, above national selfishness, we face challenges that are not going to be stopped by retreats or by exclusive rhetoric. For that and not for anything else serves Europe, ladies and gentlemen, to protect us from the temptation of smallness and to give us a powerful instrument with which to look in front of other global actors, whether they be big corporations, big economic powers... Union makes strength, and division and withdrawal make us infinitely weaker. The strength of a united Europe is born of its ability to face these challenges from civic patriotism, which is built from the communion of shared values, which appeals to an identity, which is the European one, which does not undermine at all but reinforces as complementary our Spanish, Catalan, Galician, Madridn, Murcian, Andalusian..., any of them. To give up these values, therefore, is to weaken Europe and to weaken ourselves. Let us bear this in mind precisely at this historic moment in our societies. Ladies and gentlemen, when last June I took office as President of the Government, the European Commission's task force for Brexit has been negotiating the UK withdrawal agreement for over a year. The negotiation was therefore at a very advanced stage. On the other hand, I have to tell you that no preparatory work had begun in Spain for the eventual UK exit scenario without agreement - i.e. the cliff-edge - without even a draft contingency plan made by the previous government. My main objective from the outset, ladies and gentlemen, was to ensure that the United Kingdom's decision to withdraw from the European Union had the least possible impact on citizenship and, of course, also on the companies that had invested in the United Kingdom. Look, my first participation in the European Council - that is what is called the European Council dealing with Brexit, composed of representatives of the 27 Member States without the participation of the United Kingdom - took place on 29 June 2018. [[Article 50]] At that Council, the chief negotiator of the European Commission, Mr Barnier, reported that 80% of the withdrawal agreement was already being negotiated, although in the remaining 20% there were crucial issues on which agreement had not yet been reached. Ladies and gentlemen, as I have already informed the House, I said a number of things at that European Council. First, I insisted that the rights and interests of citizens should be the high priority of negotiation. Secondly, I stressed the importance of respecting the essential principles of the European Union. And, thirdly, I warned at the time that a matter of enormous importance to our country, an issue affecting the national interest, such as Gibraltar, was still pending; I will return to this issue later. The negotiations were addressed at the informal meeting of Heads of State and Government of the European Union held in Salzburg and at the European Council in October. [[Article 50]] Finally, on 25 November last year in a European Council - another extraordinary article 50 - once I obtained the necessary guarantees on the question of Gibraltar, the European Council endorsed the withdrawal agreement and the future political declaration of the relationship between the European Union and the United Kingdom. The first of them, the agreement reached, was and is the best possible. Firstly, it preserves the rights of citizens in the United Kingdom and the British in the European Union. Secondly, it covers the commitments made and set by the financial framework. Thirdly, it provides a reasonable framework for the question of Northern Ireland and also Gibraltar. In short, we achieved a balance that laid the groundwork for a future relationship that we all want to be close and beneficial to both sides, to the European Union and to the United Kingdom. The agreement provides for a transitional period, as you know, until 31 December 2020 and extendable for two years to allow the United Kingdom and the European Union to adapt to the new situation. The European Commission and its chief negotiator, Mr Barnier, negotiated with the conviction that the difficult commitments made by the European Union would be accepted by the Member States in order to obtain an orderly Brexit, an orderly and agreed exit, but also with the belief that the text agreed by the British Government could be ratified by the trusted Parliament. Unfortunately, as we have seen in recent months, this has not happened so far. There is very little time left for 29 March, which - as you know - is the date to materialize the Brexit, and the agreement remains unratified. I have to tell you, ladies and gentlemen, that time is pressing and that the signs are nothing good. Ladies and gentlemen, the withdrawal agreement is accompanied by a political declaration laying down the main lines of the European Union's future relationship with the United Kingdom. Such a declaration reflects the common will to maintain an intense economic relationship; a fundamental objective for our country, taking into account the trade surplus we have with the United Kingdom. This document also sets out the objective of an ambitious agreement on air transport, which is fundamental to us - among other things, because many of the tourists who come to our country come from the United Kingdom - and on fisheries, where the objective of linking access to water and markets is stated, and a fisheries agreement must be concluded before the end of the transitional period. In any case, ladies and gentlemen, I would also like to convey to you that we are prepared to amend the declaration, as the Government of Spain, to make it more ambitious if the United Kingdom changes the red lines it has established for negotiation. The declaration reaffirms elements which were and are fundamental to Spain and which I have been insisting on in all the European Councils and also in this House when we discussed the subject of Brexit: firstly, respect for the autonomy of decision-making by the European Union; secondly, the integrity of the internal market; and, thirdly, - very important - respect for the four defining freedoms of the European project. Let me now turn to Gibraltar in more detail. Ladies and gentlemen, in what I have always considered to be a genuine question of state, I have taken a decision that is clear and also revealing. I maintained not only the approach of open negotiation by the previous Administration, but even the negotiating team of the previous Administration remained with this Government. Repeat: same negotiating approach and same negotiating team. That approach, ladies and gentlemen, was based on a key differentiation between the transitional period and the future relationship between the United Kingdom and the European Union. As regards the transitional period, the negotiation culminated in a protocol forming part of the withdrawal agreement supplemented by several agreements strengthening cooperation in the field of citizens' rights - which is very important for workers in the Gibraltar Field - tobacco, the environment and something fundamental such as police and customs cooperation in this area. During this period, we were pursuing two clear objectives. On the one hand, as I have told you before, the mobility of citizens and frontier workers, safeguarding their rights, and, on the other hand, the process of rationalising certain key aspects, such as police, environmental cooperation and tobacco prices, which must culminate in the negotiation on the future relationship between Spain and the European Union, linking Gibraltar, and always with a clear goal, of allowing balanced socio-economic development in the Campo de Gibraltar, which is the objective of the Government of Spain. As you know, when the text of the political declaration was published, we discovered that it did not foresee the need for any agreement affecting Gibraltar to have the approval of the Government of Spain. For this reason, I did not accept the withdrawal agreement or the future political declaration until I obtained the maximum guarantees for Spain that would ensure that the future relationship with the European Union, as regards Gibraltar, logically, goes unexcusably through Spain. The institutional shielding achieved can be described as optimal and is articulated around several elements, which I would like to share with all of you. Firstly, we have two statements, from the European Commission and the European Council, on the one hand, and from the United Kingdom, on the other, contained in letters from President Juncker and President Tusk and the British Permanent Representative rejecting the fact that Article 184 contained in that agreement applies to the content of the future relationship, including the territorial area. Secondly, we have obtained a statement from the Council and the European Commission, which shields Spain's position in the face of future negotiations. I would like to insist that this is a joint statement by the European Council and the European Commission, which is, after all, the guardian of compliance with the treaties, exclusive to the question of Gibraltar. The shielding achieved ensures Gibraltar's exclusion from negotiations for the future relationship between the European Union and the United Kingdom, something that had not happened so far, ladies and gentlemen; and, furthermore, Spain's leadership in defining the relationship that the European Union may have with Gibraltar is acknowledged, requiring Spain's consent to negotiate any implementing agreement with Gibraltar. And in case anyone has any doubts about the significance of this multiple guarantee, I would like to recall again in this House the assessments made by the various European leaders, the last of them the German Foreign Minister, who clearly say that all the agreements reached by the Spanish Government have legal validity. I am sure that some skeptics, in my words, will not doubt those expressed by other European leaders. Ladies and gentlemen, given the uncertainties surrounding the eventual ratification of the withdrawal agreement, which you have seen reflected over the last few weeks and, in particular, after the vote lost by the British Government, there is one aspect which I think is of particular relevance and which I would like to emphasise. The declaration on the future relationship recognizing the special role of Spain does not contain any mention of the withdrawal agreement. This is very important because, in other words, what we want to say is that everything we have agreed on Gibraltar will remain in force, even in the case of a non-retirement agreement or a cliff-edge. As you know, on 15 January the British Parliament rejected the withdrawal agreement by a very large majority, as I have told you before. This scenario undoubtedly increases the risk of a withdrawal without agreement. Prime Minister Theresa May announced her intention to submit to the withdrawal agreement for a second vote in mid-March, if she could get adjustments on the implementation of the backstop in Northern Ireland. The position of Spain, as I have conveyed to Prime Minister Theresa May in various telephone conversations, is the same: it is not possible to temporarily limit the backstop or a unilateral exit from it. By its very nature as a security mechanism, it is not possible to set a time limit; it is not only a question of solidarity with one of our main partners, Ireland, but also of preserving the peace that has hardly been achieved in Northern Ireland for decades. It cannot be ruled out that the British Prime Minister is requesting an extension of Article 50 of the Treaty on European Union, as the door opened yesterday to do - and I want to make it clear - in view of this possible position of the British Government, which, although Spain is not going to oppose the granting of any extension, it must have a certain prospect of resolution; that is, prolonging uncertainty by delaying deadlines is not a reasonable or desirable alternative. Ladies and gentlemen, the Spanish Government has taken into account all possible scenarios in this complex process, including, logically, the possibility of the United Kingdom leaving without the agreement of the European Union. As I pointed out at the beginning of my speech, we had to start work on almost zero preparation because the previous administration had done absolutely nothing. [[Rumors]] The report on the contingency planning of the Spanish Government was presented to the Council of Ministers on 11 January, in the face of an unagreed exit scenario from the United Kingdom of the European Union, and the national measures complement those prepared by the European Commission which naturally cover areas of Community competence. The plans are aimed at two objectives: firstly, to preserve the interests of the Spanish and British citizens who exercised their right to freedom of movement before the date of withdrawal; and secondly, to preserve the normal development of Spanish trade flows and economic interests. The measures adopted in this context include three lines of action: the first, the normative action; the second, the action of logistical preparation; and the third, communication actions. In the normative sphere, the Government has chosen the figure of the Royal Decree-Law to develop the main contingency measures. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, I announce to you the following: This next Friday, the Council of Ministers will approve the royal decree-law that will be immediately referred to the House for validation; a royal decree-law, which will be a sort of royal decree omnibus, in order to be able to deal with all the eventualities of a disordered Brexit. As you know, this is a legislative tool that allows the Government, in case of extraordinary and urgent need, to adopt rules with a law rank that must be validated within thirty days by the Congress of Deputies. Given the uncertainties they still plan, the validity of the rule will logically depend on the effective exit from the United Kingdom without an agreement adopted, in accordance with Article 50 of the Treaty on European Union. The regulation will be aligned with the contingency principles established by the European Commission, and these are: firstly - and this is important, ladies and gentlemen - contingency rules not intended to regulate definitively, but to facilitate the transit to a new situation and therefore subject to a temporary limit; and secondly, they are unilateral rules, although conditional on the United Kingdom providing treatment equivalent to Spanish nationals in that country. Therefore, temporality and unilaterality conditioned on an equivalence in the treatment. The safeguarding of the rights of Spaniards in the United Kingdom is therefore a fundamental objective for the Government of Spain, and the decree establishes various areas of action. With regard to citizenship, all young people and not so young people living in the United Kingdom are covered by measures relating to residence, cross-border workers, access to protections and public service, social security, health care and education, as well as rules on administrative and judicial cooperation in criminal matters, which relate mainly to European arrest and surrender orders issued before the date of withdrawal. In the chapter on economic activities, measures are taken in the areas of financial services - which are logically important - customs, public sector contracts, authorizations and licences; ultimately, driving licences, defence and dual-use materials, weapons, explosives and transport. Contingency measures will logically, where appropriate, extend to Gibraltar. In addition to the Royal Decree-Law, I would like to mention two other contingency measures of a normative nature. Firstly, the right to vote in municipal elections. As you know, the Council of Ministers of 18 January 2019 authorized the signing and provisional application of an agreement with the United Kingdom so that the respective nationals can exercise the rights of active and passive suffrage in the municipal elections of the other State. And secondly, aid to businesses. The Secretary of State for Trade, belonging to the Ministry of Industry, Commerce and Tourism, has empowered specific lines of aid to associations of exporters and companies affected by the Brexit. Logically, as it cannot be otherwise, we will be next to our farmers, our distributors and our entrepreneurs in this very complex transit. The second line of action to which he referred is that of the logistical nature, given the impact it would have on the needs of material and, above all, personal means, an outflow without agreement by turning the United Kingdom into a third State. With regard to personal media, I would like to say the following: the most relevant areas that require effort and reinforcement of personnel are cross-border control of persons, customs control, and external health inspection services, both for the import and export of goods. The Council of Ministers on Friday 8 February approved an agreement on measures in the field of public employment with a view to strengthening the services affected by the departure of the United Kingdom from the European Union. Among other measures, the coverage of the needs arising from the Brexit is prioritized in relation to the public job vacancies of the year 2018, and a royal decree-law is approved which advances part of the public job offer for the year 2019, allowing the immediate coverage of the new places with interim staff until the procedures are resolved. In short, ladies and gentlemen, the forecast for staff reinforcement is 1735 new staff, of which 875 are public employees or jobs of the General Administration of the State. The remaining 860 personnel relate to the coverage of services provided by public enterprises, mainly AENA and State Ports. This is in terms of human resources needs. And as for the needs of material means the same logically affect fundamentally the ports and the airports, and their attention will be facilitated, where appropriate, by the provision in the royal decree-law that their recruitment be considered emergency. In spite of the advances that, after analysing the changes that will entail the departure from the United Kingdom without agreement, the responsible departments have not appreciated the need to carry out new large civil works, and it is considered that the storage infrastructures at ports and airports for customs purposes are suitable for this new situation. The Government's third area of action is communication to citizens, businesses and other economic and institutional actors. I summarize the main initiatives in this regard. The Brexit space on the La Moncloa website has been in operation since 14 January, and it centralises all information concerning the departure of the United Kingdom from the European Union. This information is accessible to citizens and operators, both in English and Spanish. Detailed sectoral information is also included on the websites of the various ministerial departments. This on the one hand. Secondly, special business seminars have been set up, developed by the Secretariat of State for Trade with the cooperation of the European Commission itself. They report on the different scenarios and the need to take preparatory measures, and I inform you that since the end of 2018 seminars have been held in thirty-three cities in each of the autonomous communities. Therefore, web space for information and seminars specifically aimed at this issue in each and every Autonomous Community. I would also like to refer to two additional issues: the first, citizens' associations. The Spanish authorities have received, whenever requested, the associations of British citizens in Spain and Spanish residents in the United Kingdom, and maintain fluid contact with each and every one of these associations. The second is that of the autonomous, fundamental and important communities in this transit that we have to pass through. Contingency planning work is being shared with the autonomous communities - with each and every one of the autonomous governments - through the Conference on European Union-related Affairs, and the creation of a group of focal points. Ladies and gentlemen, in short, maximum institutional cooperation between the Spanish Government and the various levels of administration. Ladies and gentlemen, there are four weeks left to reach the date of 29 March, when, as you know, two years of the activation of Article 50 of the Treaty on European Union are being fulfilled, and although it is not impossible - as I have told you before - to extend the time limit laid down in that Article, some sources - the Government is working with certainties and not with speculation - offer a scenario with only two possibilities: the first is an orderly withdrawal, based on a meticulously negotiated agreement for long months, to which we have referred in various appearances before you and of which I have also made a brief summary in this intervention, and an abrupt withdrawal, without any agreement. These are the only two scenarios, and as I have explained to you the decision is not for the Government of Spain or the European Union, but for the British Government and Parliament. In any case, the Spaniards must know that, whatever the final scenario, we are prepared to face the consequences. Ladies and gentlemen, I shall now turn to the following subject which is the subject of this hearing. When nine months ago we took on the task of government, we did so with the conviction that political life needed a deep regeneration, a change that would banish not only corruption, but also disaffection and public distrust of institutions. The crisis and its aftermath only accelerated an already pressing need in our democracy, which is that of regeneration, which is an outstanding subject in our country. That is why we were always clear that exemplaryness should always be at the heart of the Government's political project. Our standard of demand was much higher from the beginning than that which had prevailed until then, let us not say with regard to the null assumption of responsibilities of the previous Government, averted by scandals of corruption and affected by a worrisome paralysis to deal with them. We are proud of this, even though we are aware that this implies greater attention to each of our steps, and also to our previous experiences of entering politics. This introduction I can say, ladies and gentlemen, that all the members of the Government have my full confidence, because each and every one of the ministers and ministers meets those standards of exemplaryity which we have imposed as an unnegotiable list. [[Applauds]] This is what the citizens demanded, and this is what we assumed as our own, aware of the events that led to the motion of censure and of our duty to promote and lead this democratic regeneration. In the cases before us, I would like to make clear four points that guide this Government's action in this area. Firstly, all the ministers and ministers of the Government of Spain comply with their tax obligations. This has been accredited by the competent bodies to establish it, and is shown by all the tax and property documents that each of them has made public. Transparency is inseparable from regeneration and the return of trust. This Government exercises it not only because of the fulfilment of a duty, but also and above all because of conviction. Secondly, all the ministers and ministers of the Government of Spain, ladies and gentlemen, use or have used legal figures in their management of income and wealth, without exception. Thirdly, these legal figures aim to manage the heritage and not to be enriched. And finally, when all three of these conditions were not met, we assumed our responsibility, regardless of the political moment and without personal considerations, without excuses and speedily. Ladies and gentlemen, I was telling you that we are aware that the level of demand has changed, we must all take care of the political and historical moment that our society is experiencing, because corruption or non-exemplarity not only harms a particular administration or agency; corruption, the lack of response to it or the inadequate and inadequate response produces a haste that goes beyond, that carves the democratic building in its entirety, that undermines the most valuable of free societies, that is trust. Ladies and gentlemen, in conclusion, I should like to refer lastly to the situation in Venezuela. I am convinced that all the groups in this House share the same desire for Venezuela, and it is a peaceful coexistence that will enable them as a country to move forward in solving all the problems they have. And I am convinced that most of the groups in this House also share the diagnosis of the path that must be taken, which are free, democratic, transparent and with all guarantees; elections that decide without any doubt who should govern Venezuela. My Government, as it cannot be otherwise, ladies and gentlemen, is following with the utmost attention the evolution of events in Venezuela, and we stand firm in our position, a position that I have just anticipated: the defence to the extreme of democratic legitimacy and the holding of free elections with all guarantees as the only possible solution to the deep crisis in the country. Venezuela has been immersed in a very serious political, economic, social and humanitarian situation, as we are seeing, which has only deteriorated alarmingly in recent months. The presidential elections of 2018, in particular in May, were neither free nor fair nor credible, and therefore Nicolás Maduro lacks all democratic legitimacy. That is what Spain said, and that is what the European Union and much of the international community said. To the progressive limitation of fundamental rights and freedoms that the Venezuelan population has been suffering, ladies and gentlemen, there is a serious deterioration in the country's institutional life, as well as the persecution of the opposition. Look, the economic and social situation in the country is dramatic - here are the images we are seeing - but I will give you some data that I think illustrate very well the gravity of the situation in Venezuela. According to the International Monetary Fund in 2018 Venezuela closed with an inflation of 1,370,000 %, and forecasts for 2019 are close to 10,000,000 % increase in inflation. Gross domestic product has fallen by 55 % over the past six years, and the International Monetary Fund estimates that 2018 will have fallen by 18 %. The imperishment, famine and destitution of essential medicines and products have led to poverty for millions of people, leading to an unprecedented migration exodus in the Latin American region. The United Nations estimates that 3.4 million Venezuelans have left the country fleeing this terrible situation. Let me stress this, almost 3 and a half million Venezuelans have marched from their homeland; some fleeing persecution, most fleeing from hunger, violence, corruption and the lack of freedoms and opportunities. This fact alone determines that the Maduro regime has failed dramatically, that no country can allow more than 10% of its population to emigrate or be exiled. Spain and the Government could not remain unfettered in this situation, we could not look elsewhere, and therefore the follow-up of the situation in Venezuela has been an absolute priority of our Government's foreign policy, for our commitment to human rights and also to the social and democratic rule of law, for our defence of democracy and for our special relationship with Latin America, with the Ibero-American Community of Nations and, in particular, with Venezuela, a country so close to which we share emotional, social and economic ties of extraordinary intensity. Moreover, the Government could not ignore the serious implications that the deterioration of the political, social and economic situation in Venezuela has for its neighbouring countries, as well as for the entire region. The Government is and has always been very aware of our great responsibility for the large Spanish colony that lives in Venezuela, more than 160,000 compatriots, whose well-being and security is our absolute priority and also logically the important presence of Spanish companies in Venezuela. This fact has always forced us and continues to force us - I mean, ladies and gentlemen - to prudence and wisdom in our manifestations and actions. The Government has acted with firmness and determination, convinced that only from democratic legitimacy, from respect for human, civil and political rights, and from deep respect for the will of Venezuelans and Venezuelans, expressed in free and guaranteed presidential elections, could the solution be found to the current situation. That is why in these nine months I would like to remind you that the Government has been taking a series of measures aimed at exponentially increasing international pressure on the Maduro regime, and at promoting a peaceful and democratic solution to the country's crisis. To mention a few milestones, on 25 June 2018 the Spanish Government supported the decision of the Foreign Affairs Council to add eleven new people to the list of sanctions, logically eleven people linked to the Maduro regime; and at the Foreign Affairs Council in November 2018 we support the decision to extend the sanctions package adopted in November 2017. On 27 September 2018, ladies and gentlemen, Spain was one of twenty-three countries that, with its favourable vote, made it possible for the UN Human Rights Council to adopt a historic resolution on Venezuela, condemning abuses and urging the Government of Caracas to accept humanitarian aid. The situation is serious and one fact is particularly revealing in this regard: in 2018 more than 20,000 Venezuelan citizens have applied for international protection in Spain. The Government has strengthened the competent administrative services to process asylum applications, and we are working to ensure that in the current legislative framework we can offer an alternative migration status to all Venezuelans who do not meet the requirements for refugee status. This Government shares the suffering of the hundreds of thousands of Venezuelans who have been forced to leave their country, and we want Spain to be a port of welcome and solidarity. Ladies and gentlemen, the political, social, economic and humanitarian situation in Venezuela has placed the international community in the face of the need to respond with strong and determined support for democratic legitimacy, represented by the National Assembly and its President Juan Guaidó. The Government has acted with the utmost political determination and also with the greatest urgency, with the conviction that the only viable solution, the only legitimate solution, the only necessary solution is through the convening, as I have told you before, of democratic presidential elections with full guarantees in Venezuela. On January 10, Spain did not send any representatives to the inauguration ceremony of Nicolás Maduro, as did the rest of the European countries, because we never recognized, as I told you before, the legitimacy of the presidential elections held on May 20. Thus, in the course of a rapid evolution of events, and following the assumption of Juan Guaidó, President of the legitimate National Assembly, as Acting President of Venezuela on 23 January, the Government of Spain assumed its political responsibilities and maintained a clear leadership position in the European Union, followed and recognized by the majority of the Member States. The Government of Spain has always worked intensively with our community partners, in line with our knowledge of the situation and our sensitivity as a member of the Ibero-American Community. Spain has always considered that joint action by the European Union was an effective element of pressure consistent with the functioning of our common foreign policy, and at the same time the Government has considered that the lack of full European consultation, which is ultimately what has been achieved, could not be an obstacle for Spain and other Member States to take the positions they consider essential at this crucial juncture. With these two ideas, with the idea of joint action within the framework of the European Union, and secondly, that in the face of a lack of full European consultation there would be countries like ours to take their positions, always seeking to act effectively in the pressure for a peaceful and democratic solution in Venezuela, and wishing to attract those positions to the greatest number of community partners, it was when I made a last call to Nicolás Maduro to call presidential elections with all the guarantees within eight days. I would like to stress, ladies and gentlemen, that the position of the Government has been clear at all times, at all times; we do not seek to put or remove governments or presidents in Venezuela, it is not for us, this is for the Venezuelan people; with our action, with our action, we want to promote the return to the full implementation of democratic procedures in Venezuela, that the people of Venezuela can choose their future, and with it the exit of this crisis through fair, free and transparent elections. [[Applauds]] We want the fate of Venezuela to arise peacefully from the free vote of its citizens. In this context, we meet as you know the eight-day deadline without calling for presidential elections by Nicolás Maduro, and the Government took a step forward, and fulfilling the commitment made in my statement of 26 January, on 4 February I again appeared to recognize Juan Guaidó officially as president-in-charge of Venezuela. Immediately after the announcement we made, and it is not by chance, France, Germany, the United Kingdom, the Netherlands and Portugal made such a recognition public. Many other Member States have followed; President Juan Guaidó has thanked us for our commitment to his democratic struggle and Spain's leadership within the European Union. The Government has also promoted - and I think it is important to stress this, ladies and gentlemen - a collective effort at the European level and at the United Nations level to assist and also to accompany in the process of the presidential elections, through the creation of an international contact group whose first meeting at ministerial level was held in Montevideo on 7 February, and which you know is composed of different members of the European Union, including Spain, and Latin American States. I would like to stress something important: that this contact group is in no way a mediation initiative, that stage has already passed; Nicolás Maduro has repeatedly demonstrated that he has no intention of dialogue, and has used successive mediation attempts as an instrument to gain time and to perpetuate himself in power. His consequent refusal to call presidential elections has been a tangible demonstration that Maduro does not want a sincere negotiation. Ladies and gentlemen, Spain has lived up to what was expected of us in this very complex and difficult situation. We have acted at all times guided by our responsibility as committed members of the Ibero-American Community, and moved also by the conviction that multilateralism and concertation reinforce our actions in the international sphere to solve this crisis. Nothing that happens in Latin America is alien to us, and Venezuela can count on the Government of Spain because we will remain fully committed to the restoration of democracy in that country, and also to absolute respect for the human rights and freedoms of the Venezuelan people. That is why we deeply regret the acts of violence that have taken place in recent days on the borders between Venezuela and Colombia and Venezuela and Brazil regarding the attempt to bring humanitarian aid to Venezuelan territory. These days we have also had the opportunity - and I hope this is a position shared by all the parliamentary groups in this House - to express our rejection of any non-peaceful solution, and in particular of any foreign military intervention in Venezuela. [[Applauds]] There are some mistakes of the past that must not be committed again, history embarrasses us, and we do not want to be ashamed again, so we will continue to work in coordination with our European partners and with our Latin American partners to promote the immediate holding of elections as soon as possible, the only legitimate way out and able to offer a permanent solution that satisfies the aspirations of the people of Venezuela. Thank you very much, Madam President. [[Prolonged applause of the ladies and gentlemen of the Socialist Parliamentary Group, and of the members of the Government, standing]]

Ten years ago, the financial system imploded, undressing a mechanism that worked by inflating speculative bubbles to absorb resources from the economy and turn them into private profit. In a few months, $34 trillion evaporated, 40% of the world's wealth. Faced with the magnitude of the disaster, Sarkozy said that capitalism had to be refounded - he said in Davos - but in Brussels they decided it was better to rescue those who had sunk it. Troika policy will pass into history as a scandal: not only did they rescue the financial system as a whole with public money, but they allowed those same agents to continue working with speculative attacks on the debt of countries in difficulty, and when the benefits of these operations have been at risk they have been rescued again. It is so obscene that it is still difficult to believe today. [[Applauds]] In Greece, they crushed a democratically elected government and ended up leaving cashiers without money to impose the austerity plan decided by Germany; that plan has devalued wages by 20%, cut pensions 14 times and cost the country 30% of its GDP and eight years of uninterrupted recession. Did it at least help to reduce the debt? No, the debt has not stopped increasing, it is now at 180% of GDP and the country in debt until 2060. So, what's it been for? So that German banks have recovered their money and earned another €3 billion in the process. If the European Union has rescued someone in Greece it has been to the German bank. [[Applauds]] In Spain, we were imposed brutal cuts in public spending, a massive devaluation of wages and pensions, two labour reforms, the largest increase in indirect taxes in Europe. Public debt had risen from 37 per cent to 100 per cent in just two years. Was it, as they said, for unsustainable social spending, for what our health, education or pensions cost, or did it have something to do with the EUR 60 billion invested in lost funds to rescue the financial system? Since then the bank has won 100 billion more, but it does not return the money; that is, we were reformed by the Constitution to ensure at all risk the interests of that debt, the debt of the banks, which costs us today 31 billion euros a year, 85 million euros a day. [[Applauds]] This is European capitalism in the age of austerity: we rescue those who sank the system, we pay the bill and we shield them the benefits to make them untouchable. These are the results. Today in Europe 1% of the population concentrates on 32% of the wealth. There are 468 people with over $1 billion each. Meanwhile, 119 million people, 24% of the population, are at risk of poverty. While VAT was rising to 21% here, 300 multinational companies paid 1% of their profits as taxes through secret agreements with Luxembourg. Brussels questions every euro of social spending, but does nothing while hiding 15% of European GDP in tax havens. Finance, ladies and gentlemen, has kidnapped democracy in Europe and is using the institutions at its service to socialise its losses and privatize its profits. [[Applauds]] And if you think it's an exaggeration, explain to me what happened to the Supreme Court's mortgage rulings or article 135 of the Constitution. This is not the result of a storm. The dismantling of the welfare state, the social state that gave meaning and identity to Europe, is an intentional political operation that crystallizes in this article 135. The State must first guarantee the benefits of financial creditors and then, if it can, pay hospitals and pensions. It is the ideological corpus of Maastricht, Lisbon, of the budgetary treaties taken to the extreme. Prohibiting state intervention in the economy, protecting strategic sectors, public banking, a consistent industrial policy, an expansive fiscal policy, is tying the state with hands and feet, preventing it from protecting its people in order to serve the same market interests that sank the system. Ladies and gentlemen, in this last debate in the legislature I would like to make some considerations. The first, Mr President, is that you chose to call elections in April. I could have done it this autumn: we would have had time to adopt relevant legislative reforms, without the haste and complications that it will entail taking them to the Permanent Chamber and in the form of real decrees. We are left with important laws, such as the law on combating lateness - fundamental for SMEs and self-employed people - or the law on gags, which should have been repealed. Therefore, Mr President, you have given priority to your electoral interest, you have not given priority to the general interest. I could have endured perfectly until this fall because for those laws - and you know it - there were majorities that would have made their approval possible. This legislature has been very marked by the Catalan agenda, by the Catalan question, by the Catalan lawsuit. The message of these years to the entire Spanish political system has been that there will be no political stability in Spain without resolving the Catalan question. Catalonia is too powerful and it has been shown during these years that it cannot be ruled by ignoring Catalonia nor can it be ruled against Catalonia. There may be absolute majorities that rule against the interests of the majorities that exist in Catalonia and that continues to generate political instability in the Spanish state. Ladies and gentlemen, Catalonia's conflict with state institutions goes beyond the conflict directly linked to the 2010 Constitutional Court ruling. It is a very profound conflict, very deep, very structural, that has to do basically with questions of recognition and power: with recognizing the national character of Catalonia, its status as a nation, its affirmation as a nation, and with a demand for power, political power to respond to the challenges of Catalan society according to the preferences that this society has. This demand for power and recognition explains from the end of the 19th century until now why regionalism, nationalism, federalism, sovereignism, independence and autonomy have been posing a political battle for power and recognition of Catalonia. There have been historical moments in the history of Spain in which a partial and satisfactory response has been given to these demands: in 1914, with the Commonwealth; in 1931, with the Republic and the Statute; in 1978, with democracy and the Statute. These historical moments have always ended up failing on the right: in 1936, in 1923, in 2010. They have always ended up failing by the right, because the Spanish right is extremely nationalist, loaded with an authoritarian tone, and is unable to imagine Spain in all its national, cultural and linguistic plurality. Ladies and gentlemen, we need a moderate right, a liberal right, which is willing to address this conflict from the political dialogue. Today it is not and is not expected, and there I would like to quote Michael Ignatieff, who says that for democracies to work, politicians must respect the difference between an enemy and an adversary; an adversary is someone you want to defeat, an enemy is someone you want to destroy. Ladies and gentlemen, when you talk about permanent 155, when you talk about illegalizing political parties and civil society organisations, when you talk about banning subsidies to the Democratic Party or Republican Left, you want to destroy us, you don't want to win us in the ballot box, you don't want to convince voters that your proposals are better, you want to destroy us, they are the highest expression of anti-politics and anti-democracy. And Mr President, the Spanish left was in the 1931 and 1978 agreements. He was, wasn't he? Yes, he was, and my group does not regret having voted for him in that motion of censure, but the Spanish left is contaminated or even forms part of his being, of that Jacobin and French look of the state that denies that plurality, and you again - and it has not been the first time that has passed in the Socialist Party - have been afraid of that right and have been afraid of those archaic conceptions that exist in your world and have not deepened the political dialogue, which in this legislature we have had the opportunity to consolidate. It was a shame, Mr President of the Government. In any case, we must remember once again - we have said this from this rostrum on many occasions - that the cause of Catalonia's freedom is the cause of democracy in Spain, that what explains what happened in the years 1914, 1931 and 1978 is that link by which there cannot be claimed to be democracy in Spain denying the right to freedom of Catalonia and the price that the Spanish State is paying for denying that freedom that is claimed by Catalonia is very high in terms of rights and freedoms, separation of powers, independence from the judiciary, and we are seeing it these days in the trial that is being held in the Supreme Court, in which it is evidenced - and yesterday the intervention of Jordi Cuixart has noted - the dignity of the cause that is defended by Catalonia and the absolute weakness of the legal arguments of the Attorney General's Office and the State's Advocate against Catalonia. Ladies and gentlemen, only political dialogue and willingness to agree can resolve the conflict. In 2010, with President Maragall, there was a hand in that dialogue. In April 2014, when Jordi Turull, today tried, Marta Rovira, today in exile, and Joan Herrera defended in this House a pact on a referendum, we again reached out. Before and after November 9, 2014, President Mas reached out, and before and after October 1, the Government of Catalonia, with Puigdemont and Junqueras, reached out. ParlaMint-ES_2019-02-27-CD190227.u318	We then proceed to address the appeals addressed to the Government. First, urgent inquiry by the Parliamentary Group of the Republican Left on the guarantee of the right to housing in the Spanish State. Mr. Margall Sastre has the floor for his defence.
ParlaMint-ES_2019-02-27-CD190227.u319	Thank you, President. Deputies, deputies, good afternoon, minister. The Parliamentary Group of Republican Esquerra wanted to make the last question of this legislature to talk about housing, at a critical time for many families in the Spanish state who are about to be evicted because they are unable to pay their mortgages or the increase in the price of rent. You know very well, minister, that in this country we have moved from the mortgage bubble to the rental bubble. The result remains homeless people, empty floors and wild evictions. We saw it last Friday at the 11th Argumosa Street eviction, which was carried out despite the repeated suspension requirements of the UN DEC Committee. The result is devastating: four families were evicted, one with a one-month-old child, and several people were arrested while trying to prevent it. Is this the housing model of the PSOE government, minister? Doesn't it embarrass you, as the minister responsible for housing, that these events take place? From here we want to give all our support to the broken families of Argumosa Street. Statistics show that in this country many citizens spend most of their wages paying their housing, in some cases more than 60% of their income. Meanwhile, the gentlemen of the PP and of Citizens seem a luxury to charge 900 euros of interprofessional minimum wage, when in most of the cities of this country it does not even arrive to pay the rent. We are expelling people from the rental market and reinvigorating the purchase of housing through mortgages, knowing what happened eleven years ago when the bubble burst. It is an irresponsibility and a violation of citizens' rights. You still haven't solved the regulation of the pay-as-you-go. We're throwing people out of their house while we leave them with a debt in the current account. This is a true immorality and there is no person who can defend it without the face of shame falling. The gentrification that is causing the rental bubble is destroying our neighborhoods and is throwing our neighbors out of the city centre. We're turning the center of big cities into theme parks, and it doesn't make any sense. In some neighbourhoods of large and medium-sized cities in the state, rents are rising by more than 60%, and in some cases prices are doubling or tripled when new contracts are signed. We can do many things to solve it: social rents, public housing, price regulation, taxes on empty floors. These are not Bolivarian and communist measures, but are being done by our northern European neighbours. The disproportionate increase in rents in recent years is due to multiple causes, including the unwillingness of the different PP and PSOE governments to promote the creation of a sufficient public housing stock to be able to influence prices. At present the public housing stock in Spain accounts for only 2.5% of the total, far from the values above 15% of European countries. During the period of economic growth, high fiscal incentives were introduced to buy, ahead of rent, and instead, when the economic crisis broke out and many families found it difficult to deal with their mortgages, the Government chose to rescue the banks rather than the families themselves. At that time a great opportunity was lost to create a large public housing park with a mixed model of property and affordable rents, and instead speculation was facilitated to financial institutions. The dynamism of the big cities, partly explained by tourism activity, has led to an increase in the prices of the real estate market that is difficult to assume for families increasingly punished by a precarious labour market, which continues to press down wages. This is where the necessary role of the public administration acquires value, in correcting a dysfunction of the market. The Government's decree of urgent measures in the area of housing and rent showed limitations which we consider to be inexhaustible, given the current situation, and we offered to work from now on for a new proposal which ambitiously addresses the shortcomings of the last decree. While we could consider it as a starting point for further progress in price regulation, we believe that price regulation cannot be delayed any longer. It is therefore necessary to provide cities with the necessary tools to enable them to regulate the price on the basis of their peculiarities, for example, by means of taxation instruments directly linked to the rent price or to the situation of unoccupied housing. There are some measures that we believe should be taken into account to solve this serious problem, and that we believe they should be included in the new decree they are preparing. I briefly summarize them, and we reach out to them to reach agreements. Prohibition of evictions without housing alternatives; the suffering that produces the more than sixty thousand launches per year will not be avoided with a month of moratorium on execution. It is essential that Law 24/2015, adopted in the Parliament of Catalonia and ratified by the Constitutional Court, be implemented in the rest of the State. Prohibition of evictions with an open date. Affordable rentals; it is very important, and urgent, to regulate rental prices between contract and contract, based on indices that take into account the purchasing power of the citizenry. Price increases are already regulated in various ways in countries such as Denmark, Austria, Sweden, Germany, Holland, Ireland and Italy. In addition, increases under the same contract must be closely linked to the CPI. With regard to the vulture funds, it is imperative to change the tax regime of the Socimis, financial vehicles that enjoy enormous fiscal privileges without any counterpart, and that allow the funds to grab and speculate massively with our homes, operating as a spearhead of the bubble. Finally, the mobilization of empty housing in a housing emergency context and in a country with 3.4 million empty housing, according to the latest census. I believe that the conclusion we should draw is that we cannot put patches on a serious problem such as housing, especially public and regulated housing, if we do not tackle the fundamental problem of market regulation. It's over, President. Minister, we have once again reached out to you in order to solve this very serious problem, and I look forward to your response to our interpretation. [[Applauds]]
ParlaMint-ES_2019-02-27-CD190227.u320	Thank you very much, Mr. Margall. The Minister for Development then has the floor on behalf of the Government.
ParlaMint-ES_2019-02-27-CD190227.u321	Thank you very much, Mr President. Thank you very much, Mr. Margall. To the clear and direct question that you asked me at the beginning of your speech, if this was PSOE's policy, when I was commenting on last Friday's evictions, I will tell you that obviously that is not PSOE's policy; PSOE's policy is based precisely on preventing those dramas. As for whether I'm ashamed, I'd say something else. The indignation that these dramatic situations actually produce is also accompanied by a sense of frustration as a public representative unable to carry out measures to prevent that, and I believe that this is to a large extent what I was trying to do when I presented the decree for which I did not get the support of the House. That defeat I did not see as such, even though the headlines spoke of political defeat; I lived it in another way, I lived it as a certain frustration and impotence with regard to the mandate we have, which is to try to solve people's problems and not measure ourselves around our possibilities. But I too have the constructive and positive spirit, because in the end we come here to overcome these other drawbacks that I was commenting on. Therefore my purpose is to try to fix it, to try to seek the greatest agreement so that these situations do not happen again. I can assure you that housing policy has been and continues to be one of the priorities of this Government, even at this particular moment, in which we are faced with a forthcoming call for general elections. Indeed, this Government has been working since the first day to put on the table concrete improvements that will transform the reality that we are in on our arrival. The necessary measures for the right to housing have played a prominent role in the policies of the Ministry. We also wanted him to have the treatment that deserves the importance of the subject. For this we have encouraged some of the actions that I am going to comment on. The first is that we constitute an interministerial working group for the development of urgent measures in the area of housing and rent, to coordinate with other administrations the necessary measures in the area of housing and rent. This working group was born with the priority mission of detailing the diagnosis and promoting short-, medium- and long-term measures that would enable us to develop our housing policy in depth and with adequate solidity. Secondly, we have signed the partnership agreements for the implementation of the State Housing Plan 2018-2021 with the Autonomous Communities and the cities of Ceuta and Melilla. This allowed the autonomous administrations to call for aid from the various programmes covered by the plan. In compliance with these agreements, we transfer the amount committed to each of the Autonomous Communities and to the Autonomous Cities. The Ministry of Development has ordered the payment of a total of more than 328 million euros to meet the housing subsidies of the State Housing Plan 2018-2021. We have also launched the 20 000 Housing Plan for Affordable or Social Rental. It was necessary to start with the creation and rehabilitation of a rental housing stock, making all available means. This necessarily implied a reconsideration of an essential instrument with which the Ministry of Development, such as the Soil Business Public Entity, Sepes, had practically abandoned residential promotion activity over the past few years. Last year we transferred 21.5 million euros to Sepes to start with the drive of this housing park, and to promote the construction of more than 5000 houses on Sepes land to be used for social or affordable rent. In this year 2019 we have begun the processing of a budgetary amendment to equip Sepes with an amount of 24.5 million euros. More than 1500 affordable and social rents have to be added to these 5000 housing units, the financing of which the Ministry of Development has committed itself to participate, within the framework of the State Housing Plan 2018-2021 and whose management has already begun. To this end, the Ministry of Development has signed agreements with eight autonomous communities that have wanted to participate in this increase of the housing park for rent; in particular with the communities of Andalusia, Asturias, Balearic Islands, Castile and León, Catalonia, Extremadura, Madrid and Murcia. On the other hand, we adopted a real decree-law of urgent measures in the area of housing and rent - which unfortunately, as you know, was not validated by this House - with the aim of finding a balance between three fundamental variables: firstly, to provide the tenants and tenants with a situation of balance of strength between the two parties; secondly, to provide sufficient legal certainty to the market, and thirdly, to create the conditions necessary to increase the existence of a larger rental housing stock. This Royal Decree-Law improved the regulatory framework for rent, amended the Civil Prosecution Act to ensure that social services play an essential role in judicial proceedings, amended the Horizontal Property Act to promote accessibility of homeowners'communities, and regulated fiscal and economic aspects to enable housing actions by local entities. It was a change that would help remove barriers and alleviate the serious social housing deficit in our country. In addition, as a demonstration of the rigour with which we wanted to take on all these matters, last September we published the Rental Bulletin, the Housing and Soil Observatory, promoting access to information and transparency in this market. This newsletter aims to provide information on an activity that has traditionally been marked by a smaller volume of data and information if compared to the sales market. For its elaboration, we request information from the Tax Administration, the General Council of Notaries, the College of Property Registrars, as well as the administrative records of leases or bond deposits, which operate in accordance with the laws of some autonomous communities. We integrate all these available data from the main official statistical sources to improve knowledge of the current situation and to continuously monitor the evolution of the residential sector. We have also drawn up the Spanish Urban Agenda, which we raised for consideration to the Council of Ministers last Friday. It is a non-policy strategic document that aligns with the international commitments undertaken by Spain through the 2030 Agenda, the new United Nations Urban Agenda and the Urban Agenda for the European Union. It has been the realization of a commitment by the Government to achieve equitable, just and sustainable urban development that takes into account all the towns and cities of Spain, regardless of their size and population, and under the triple prism of economic, social and environmental sustainability. With it we have wanted to respond to the challenges and needs that the world of today poses to us. We have also started work on improving financing through ICO, with a view to improving access to credit for the promotion of rented housing, rehabilitation, regeneration and urban renewal, exploring collaborative formulas with ICO to create specific funding lines to develop these activities. We also believe that there is a need to further strengthen collaboration in this area with all financial institutions. Another work that we have begun in the ministry is the development of a state law on housing, with the collaboration of a group of experts. It is necessary to develop a basic legal regime that responds to the social role of housing, configuring social housing as a public service that guarantees the equality of Spaniards in the exercise of their right to housing throughout the national territory. As you know, the Constitutional Court has accused the State of resorting to the housing laws that are being adopted by the autonomous communities, without the State itself having yet to set the basic and uniform parameters that should set the constitutional limit of those laws. Thus, never before, despite the exclusive competence of the autonomous communities in the area of housing, has it been so timely to face the task of drafting a basic state law on housing, ensuring compliance with constitutional principles and competences. Thus, the draft that we started was intended to regulate for the whole Spanish territory the basic conditions that guarantee equality in the exercise of rights and in the fulfilment of constitutional duties related to housing, as well as to establish a uniform regulation of the most essential aspects of public housing policies as a fundamental economic sector. Ladies and gentlemen, I conclude. In short, and as I have said, we have implemented a social housing policy, promoting measures to facilitate the enjoyment of decent and adequate housing. We hope to be able to continue in the next parliamentary term, but we do not give up what is left of it so that we can put in place measures to alleviate and solve the urgent problems of citizens in the area of housing. There is no doubt that all of them must be accompanied by additional measures, in which this Government, as I say to you, continues to work. And as we want to continue taking further steps, we have offered on numerous occasions, the President of the Government and myself, in this House, both you and the other groups present here, to reach a state pact for the social role of housing in which all these measures are included and can be agreed. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2019-02-27-CD190227.u328	Thank you very much, Vice President. Minister, ministers, ladies and gentlemen, our question - the last of this parliamentary term - concerns an issue that we attach great importance to. I would like to remind you that almost two years ago we presented a proposal for a law which has not reached a good place, but which, in any case, implies the recognition of a reality. Businesses, especially small and medium-sized ones, and self-employed people have a lot of difficulties - one of the most obvious and the most problematic is the slowness of business - difficulties which, together with others - the excessive funding, the lack of capitalisation - were decisive at the time of a major crisis. Many companies that would otherwise have been able to survive perfectly, due to the lateness, because their customers were late in paying, encountered an important treasury problem that conditioned their present and future viability. Ladies and gentlemen, this has been settled. There was an important initiative in this regard, the first transposition of the European directive in 2004, which improved the situation in relation to a bad one. There have also been various legislative amendments, some of which have also resulted from the transposition of the directive, but we still believe that the situation today is precarious. Before yesterday, data were presented by the Multisectoral Platform against Morosity, which I will then remind you of, and the reality is that the improvement in payment deadlines over the last few years, together with an improvement in the economic situation, has made this problem not perhaps so latent, but rather fundamental, because, Minister, if there is to be a rebound or a slowdown in our economy there will be businesses and self-employed people who are going to have a very bad time. Law 3/2004, on combating late payment, laid down certain payment periods which should be respected, but which have not been respected during these fifteen years. It also introduced measures that required improvement. If these payment periods were not respected, the creditor could claim the relevant interest, but the reality is that in 86 % of the cases such interest is not claimed. In addition, compensation can be claimed for the cost of charging unpaid amounts, which in 94% of cases is not claimed, because there is a dependency on some customers or large customers and the business relationship is put at risk if interest on delay or compensation is required. The reality is that small and medium-sized enterprises and self-employed people do not end up demanding these compensations. Therefore, from our perspective, it is absolutely necessary that a sanctioning regime be put in place within the competent administration - in our case, the autonomous communities - in order to have the obligation, the power and the possibility of establishing a gradation of sanctions. We - as we recalled - had put this bill in the Senate, in June 2015, and Citizens had done it in Congress, but the complexity and management of legislative initiatives and perhaps the lack of agility of the different tables have made it impossible today. I reminded him that the Multisectoral Platform for Combating Morosity has presented 2018 data this week. This is the average; there will be those paying with less term and cases in which the term is not 81 days, but 100, 120, 160 and 180 days. In the public sector there is also a 3-day rebound. Specifically, SMEs with employees currently account for 46% of our businesses, employ more than 8 million workers and account for 53% of total business employment.
